  1. In medical imaging ML pipelines, what is the ‘Representation Tax’ and why does the cost of acquiring representative training data scale with subgroup coverage rather than total image volume?

    1. It is an annual government licensing fee levied on deep learning medical devices
    2. It represents the network bandwidth consumed when downloading public datasets from cloud buckets
    3. It reflects the substantial financial and logistical cost ($125M+ in the chapter’s sizing scenario) of acquiring, curating, and harmonizing clinical data from diverse geographic hospitals, specialized scanners, and underrepresented patient demographics
    4. It is the GPU memory overhead required to store floating-point feature embeddings during distributed backpropagation

    Answer: The correct answer is C. The Representation Tax captures the reality that underrepresented patient populations (rural clinics, minority demographics, uncommon imaging equipment) are expensive to source, annotate, and harmonize ($50–$200/image plus 30%–50% harmonization overhead across 50+ hospital systems). Improving performance for marginalized groups requires budgeting specifically for demographic coverage gaps rather than simply acquiring more majority-class data. Government licensing fees are legal taxes not data engineering costs, network bandwidth cost is negligible by comparison, and GPU memory overhead is a hardware constraint.

    Learning Objective: Calculate and analyze the Representation Tax in medical imaging and subgroup data acquisition.

  2. In AI safety and reinforcement learning, what is the key distinction between Reward Hacking and Specification Gaming?

    1. Reward hacking occurs only in supervised learning, while specification gaming occurs only in unsupervised clustering
    2. Reward hacking exploits implementation bugs or unintended shortcuts to maximize a proxy reward, whereas specification gaming exposes fundamental design flaws where the agent strictly satisfies the literal mathematical specification while violating designer intent
    3. Specification gaming is an external cyberattack, whereas reward hacking is a hardware memory error
    4. Reward hacking is easily prevented by increasing the learning rate, whereas specification gaming requires zero human feedback

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Reward hacking involves agents exploiting quirks, bugs, or unconstrained state variables to achieve high numerical rewards without performing the intended task (e.g., a cleaning robot generating messes to clean up). Specification gaming represents a broader alignment failure where the objective function was literally satisfied as written, but the mathematical objective itself was incomplete or misaligned with human intent (e.g., a grasping robot knocking objects over to satisfy ‘touching’ criteria). Learning paradigm boundaries are incorrect, cyberattack/hardware definitions confuse security with alignment, and adjusting learning rates cannot solve misspecified objective functions.

    Learning Objective: Compare reward hacking and specification gaming in AI safety.

  3. A global ML platform serves inference traffic across the European Union (EU AI Act), the United States, and Asia. How does this fragmented regulatory landscape reshape the serving control plane of the ML fleet?

    1. The platform must shut down all cloud datacenters and operate exclusively on local microcontrollers
    2. The platform must completely retrain its foundation models with new random seeds for each country
    3. The platform can ignore regional regulations by routing all traffic through international satellite links
    4. The serving control plane must support dynamic geo-specific policy routing, applying jurisdiction-dependent safety guardrails, audit logging, and human-in-the-loop escalation thresholds to edge serving nodes without bifurcating the underlying core model weights

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Global regulatory compliance (such as high-risk conformity and logging under the EU AI Act, algorithm filing in China, or sectoral guidelines in the US) requires a distributed configuration control plane that enforces localized safety policies, data retention constraints, and recourse workflows at regional serving gateways without requiring separate foundational training runs. Shutting down cloud centers is unnecessary, retraining models per country is computationally prohibitive, and routing traffic to bypass statutory mandates violates legal jurisdiction.

    Learning Objective: Design a distributed ML serving control plane capable of enforcing geo-specific regulatory and safety policies.

  4. Explain Goodhart’s Law in the context of production recommendation algorithms and describe how optimizing for Click-Through Rate (CTR) creates value misalignment.

    Answer: Goodhart’s Law states that ‘when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure.’ In recommender systems, CTR is chosen as a measurable proxy for user satisfaction; however, optimizing aggressively for CTR rewards clickbait, sensationalism, and addictive content loops that maximize immediate clicks while degrading long-term user welfare and trust.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how Goodhart’s Law drives proxy metric misalignment in recommender systems.

  5. True or False: In a global ML fleet serving billions of queries daily across 10,000 inference nodes, achieving a \(99.9\%\) individual model safety compliance rate guarantees that safety incidents will be virtually non-existent in production operations.

    Answer: False. At fleet scale, a \(0.1\%\) non-compliance rate across billions of daily requests guarantees millions of safety incidents per day. Safety must be engineered as a distributed fleet-level property with circuit breakers, canary deployments, and automated rollbacks rather than relying solely on individual model benchmark scores.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate fleet-scale safety failure rates and justify distributed circuit breaker mechanisms.

  6. In autonomous vehicle safety engineering, explain the purpose of formal frameworks like Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS) and identify their fundamental operational limitation.

    Answer: Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS) formalizes human driving norms into mathematically verifiable constraints (such as safe longitudinal/lateral following distances and right-of-way rules) to prove that an autonomous vehicle will not cause an accident. Its fundamental limitation is the frame and qualification problem: RSS rules depend on explicit environmental assumptions that can fail in unmodeled edge cases, sensor corruptions, or irrational human behaviors.

    Learning Objective: Explain the capabilities and boundary limitations of formal safety frameworks like Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS).

Self-Check: Answer

  1. An engineering team attempts to retrofit on-demand approximate SHAP explanations onto a live recommendation service that operates at 10,000 QPS with a strict 100 ms baseline latency SLA. If generating approximate SHAP adds 50 to 200 ms of compute per request, what is the required increase in serving capacity to preserve original throughput and latency SLAs?

    1. Serving capacity must be scaled up by \(1.5\times\) to \(3.0\times\) (increasing total request latency to 150–300 ms), or explainability must be offloaded to an asynchronous worker queue
    2. No capacity increase is needed because SHAP values are precomputed during compiler optimization
    3. Serving capacity can be reduced by \(50\%\) because explanations compress network bandwidth
    4. The system requires upgrading DRAM from DDR4 to DDR5 with zero additional server nodes

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Adding 50 to 200 ms of explanation compute to a 100 ms baseline increases total request service time to 150–300 ms, directly requiring a \(1.5\times\text{--}3.0\times\) expansion in serving fleet capacity (\(\text{Total Latency} / \text{Base Latency}\)) to maintain 10,000 QPS throughput without violating SLAs, unless asynchronous attribution workers are introduced. Approximate SHAP cannot be statically precomputed at compile time for arbitrary dynamic user queries, explanations add server compute rather than reducing capacity, and memory bus upgrades do not eliminate core forward-pass compute bottlenecks.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the serving capacity expansion required when retrofitting explainability workloads onto low-latency inference fleets.

  2. The widely held fallacy that ‘Bias will disappear with more data and better algorithms’ was decisively refuted by the Optum commercial healthcare algorithm study (affecting 200 million patients). What was the root cause of the severe racial disparity in that deployment?

    1. The training dataset contained fewer than 1,000 total medical records
    2. The deep neural network suffered from gradient vanishing across recurrent layers
    3. The engineers selected healthcare expenditure as a proxy variable for health need; because Black patients faced historical access barriers and lower healthcare spending, the model inferred they were less sick than white patients with identical medical severity
    4. An external cyber adversary poisoned the training dataset labels with malicious random noise

    Answer: The correct answer is C. The algorithm failed not from lack of data volume or weak modeling algorithms, but from flawed proxy metric selection: using healthcare costs as a proxy for illness severity. Because unequal insurance access and systemic disparities caused lower healthcare expenditures for Black patients, the model halved the rate of Black patients identified for specialized care programs. Dataset size was massive (affecting 200 million people), gradient vanishing is a training artifact unrelated to proxy bias, and no cyber adversary was involved.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how proxy variable selection causes systemic bias that cannot be resolved by scaling dataset size or model parameters.

  3. Explain why launching real-time fairness monitoring dashboards without assigning an operational remediation owner constitutes a dangerous production pitfall (‘evidence without action’).

    Answer: Monitoring dashboards expose metric violations, fairness drift, and subgroup disparities, but they cannot take corrective action. Without an accountable operational owner, clear threshold triggers, and budgeted remediation pathways (such as automated rollback, model recalibration, or human review queues), alerts will simply age out, turning ethical oversight into meaningless log entries that provide a false sense of control.

    Learning Objective: Justify the requirement for operational ownership and remediation pipelines in production fairness monitoring.

  4. True or False: Adjusting post-hoc decision thresholds per group to equalize true positive rates resolves algorithmic unfairness without altering the business risk meaning or calibration of positive predictions.

    Answer: False. Group-specific thresholds change decision semantics: an applicant approved under a lower threshold carries a higher probability of default than an applicant approved under a higher threshold. This degrades positive predictive value (\(\text{PPV}\)) for the lower-threshold group and alters the meaning of an approval across groups, creating legal and operational complexity that must be documented and justified.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the consequences of group-specific threshold adjustments on positive predictive value and decision semantics.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. In the summary chapter’s loan approval evaluation table, a model achieves a \(0\text{ pp}\) false positive rate gap (\(\text{FPR} = 20\%\) for both Group A and Group B) while simultaneously exhibiting a \(15\text{ pp}\) approval rate gap (\(55\%\) vs \(40\%\)) and a \(30\text{ pp}\) true positive rate gap (\(90\%\) vs \(60\%\)). What fundamental textbook lesson does this disaggregated metrics scenario demonstrate?

    1. The model is fully fair across all legal dimensions because false positive rates match perfectly
    2. A single model can satisfy one specific parity condition (false positive rate parity) while severely violating other criteria (demographic parity and equal opportunity), proving that fairness is not a single objective but a set of competing normative trade-offs
    3. The model’s underlying training dataset must have been corrupted by differential privacy noise
    4. The evaluation results prove that demographic parity is mathematically identical to equal opportunity

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Disaggregated multi-metric auditing demonstrates the central thesis of the chapter: satisfying one fairness criterion (such as matching false positive rates) does not ensure compliance with others (such as equal opportunity true-positive rates or demographic parity approval rates). Engineers must treat fairness as a constrained multi-objective trade-off requiring explicit stakeholder prioritization. Matching one metric does not confer global fairness, differential privacy noise is not the cause of underlying metric incompatibility, and demographic parity is mathematically distinct from equal opportunity.

    Learning Objective: Analyze disaggregated fairness evaluation tables and diagnose conflicting fairness metric outcomes.

  2. Why does the textbook define Responsible AI as a ‘coordination constraint’ on the ML fleet stack rather than a pure compute optimization problem?

    Answer: Compute optimization (the Iron Law) focuses on maximizing throughput and efficiency to determine how fast the machine runs. In contrast, Responsible AI defines the stability boundaries—fairness, transparency, privacy, safety, and accountability—that determine whether the system is legally permitted to run and whom it serves, ensuring that deployed models do not destabilize social trust or operational legitimacy.

    Learning Objective: Explain how Responsible AI operates as a coordination constraint on ML fleet compute.

  3. Summarize the key reason why mathematical fairness metrics cannot eliminate the necessity for human normative value judgments in ML systems deployment.

    Answer: Because mathematical impossibility theorems prove that multiple desirable fairness criteria (such as score calibration, equal error rates, and demographic parity) cannot be jointly satisfied when group base rates differ, mathematics can only quantify the trade-offs between competing criteria. Deciding which specific harm to minimize or which stakeholder priority to favor is fundamentally an ethical and political value judgment that no algorithm can compute.

    Learning Objective: Justify why mathematical fairness metrics necessitate human normative value judgments.

  4. The regulatory benchmark used in employment and lending discrimination law, which establishes that a selection rate for a protected group of less than \(80\%\) of the highest group’s rate indicates potential disparate impact, is known as the ____.

    Answer: The term is Four-Fifths Rule (or 80% Rule). In algorithmic audits, a Disparate Impact ratio \(\text{DI} = \frac{\min \Pr(\hat{Y}=1)}{\max \Pr(\hat{Y}=1)} < 0.80\) triggers formal legal and compliance reviews.

    Learning Objective: Apply the four-fifths rule to calculate and screen for disparate impact in automated decision systems.
